    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… they told me I had to come in. From the station. I’m an EMT, and there was this thing in the bay with the airway bags, and I know it probably sounds like I was just being difficult, but I wasn’t trying to be.

I kept moving the airway bags into this pattern—like, the order was wrong, and I could tell it was wrong. I had to get them lined up the way I knew was right. And while I was doing it I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, because they fit with it, the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away from the bags, but I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it. It felt like if I broke it off before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.

So… they sent me here after that.

Yeah. I mean… yes, it felt like I had to. It wasn’t just me wanting things neat or trying to be stubborn with my lieutenant. The bags were in the wrong order, and once I noticed it, I couldn’t leave them like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.

The pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.

So yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.

I don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.

And then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.

I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.

I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.

So when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.

I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.

So I just kept doing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.

They tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.

And outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.

So yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.

I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.

At the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, not because I thought that was a normal thing to do, exactly, but because it felt connected. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.

When my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. But it felt imposed, yeah — not by him, but by the whole situation, like if I broke the sequence or left before it was finished, the bay would go bad somehow. Unsafe, wrong, out of order. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. I know I’m the one doing it. I can see my own hands moving the bags, I can hear myself saying the dispatch numbers out loud, so it’s not like I think someone else is physically making me do it. But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”

At the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.

So I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.

It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like they're being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, not because I thought that was a normal thing to do, exactly, but because it felt connected. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.\n\nWhen my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. But it felt imposed, yeah — not by him, but by the whole situation, like if I broke the sequence or left before it was finished, the bay would go bad somehow. Unsafe, wrong, out of order. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are a part of who you are, or do they feel foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. I know I’m the one doing it. I can see my own hands moving the bags, I can hear myself saying the dispatch numbers out loud, so it’s not like I think someone else is physically making me do it. But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F42.2", "F42.8", "F60.5"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have recurring thoughts that you cannot control?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I get thoughts stuck in my head, yes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by recurring thoughts I can’t control.

Like sometimes it’s less of a thought with words and more like a pressure or a certainty that something isn’t right, and then I keep coming back to it even when I’m trying not to. I’ll tell myself, “Just leave it alone,” and then my mind keeps circling back anyway. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding to think about it. It sort of grabs my attention and won’t let me move on.

But if you mean, like, one specific sentence or image that repeats over and over every day, I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say it that neatly. It’s more like my brain keeps returning to the same problem until I do something about it, and even then sometimes it doesn’t really feel finished. So… yes, maybe, but I’m not totally sure I’m using the right words.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… they told me I had to come in. From the station. I’m an EMT, and there was this thing in the bay with the airway bags, and I know it probably sounds like I was just being difficult, but I wasn’t trying to be.\n\nI kept moving the airway bags into this pattern—like, the order was wrong, and I could tell it was wrong. I had to get them lined up the way I knew was right. And while I was doing it I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, because they fit with it, the sequence. My lieutenant kept telling me to stop and step away from the bags, but I couldn’t just stop in the middle of it. It felt like if I broke it off before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nSo… they sent me here after that.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to move the airway bags into that specific pattern?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, it felt like I had to. It wasn’t just me wanting things neat or trying to be stubborn with my lieutenant. The bags were in the wrong order, and once I noticed it, I couldn’t leave them like that.\n\nI kept thinking there was a right pattern, like the numbers from dispatch fit into it somehow, so I was saying them out loud while I moved the bags. And when he told me to stop, I heard him, I did, but stepping away before the sequence was finished felt… dangerous, I guess. Like the whole bay would go bad or be wrong if I broke it right then. So I just kept doing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like they're being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.\n\nWhen my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are a part of who you are, or do they feel foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. ---------- turn 11 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

[
  {

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like something happening to me, honestly. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, so I don’t want to make it sound like I blacked out or like someone else picked up my arms. But it doesn’t feel like a normal decision either.

That’s why they sent me here, really. At the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern, and at the time it felt like I knew it was the right pattern. Not just that I preferred it or wanted things neat — it was like there was a correct arrangement and I had to get it there. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, over and over, because somehow the numbers felt tied to where the bags were supposed to go.

My lieutenant told me to stop and step away, and I heard him. I understood he was giving me an order. But I couldn’t make myself leave it alone, because it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad. Not “bad” in a normal way I can explain, but wrong, unsafe, like the place wouldn’t be right anymore.

So… no, it doesn’t feel like “me” in the way I think of myself. I don’t want to be standing there ignoring my lieutenant and repeating numbers. But in the moment it feels so strong that it’s almost like it’s happening through me, and I’m trying to keep up with it.

So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. The bags were in the wrong order, and once I noticed it, I couldn’t leave them like that.\n\nI kept thinking there was a right pattern, like the numbers from dispatch fit into it somehow, so I was saying them out loud while I moved the bags. And when he told me to stop, I heard him, I did, but stepping away before the sequence was finished felt… dangerous, I guess. Like the whole bay would go bad or be wrong if I broke it right then. So I just kept doing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like they're being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, not because I thought that was a normal thing to do, exactly, but because it felt connected. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.\n\nWhen my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. But it felt imposed, yeah — not by him, but by the whole situation, like if I broke the sequence or left before it was finished, the bay would go bad somehow. Unsafe, wrong, out of order. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are a part of who you are, or do they feel foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. I know I’m the one doing it. I can see my own hands moving the bags, I can hear myself saying the dispatch numbers out loud, so it’s not like I think someone else is physically making me do it. But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you cannot control?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I’ll tell myself, “Just leave it alone,” and then my mind keeps circling back anyway. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding to think about it. It sort of grabs my attention and won’t let me move on.\n\nBut if you mean, like, one specific sentence or image that repeats over and over every day, I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say it that neatly. It’s more like my brain keeps returning to the same problem until I do something about it, and even then sometimes it doesn’t really feel finished. So… yes, maybe, but I’m not totally sure I’m using the right words.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of you or something that is happening to you against your will?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something happening to me, honestly. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, so I don’t want to make it sound like I blacked out or like someone else picked up my arms. But it doesn’t feel like a normal decision either.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, really. At the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern, and at the time it felt like I knew it was the right pattern. I understood he was giving me an order. But I couldn’t make myself leave it alone, because it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad. Not “bad” in a normal way I can explain, but wrong, unsafe, like the place wouldn’t be right anymore.\n\nSo… no, it doesn’t feel like “me” in the way I think of myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Yeah, it is. I mean, if I’m being honest, that’s probably the worst part — not just that I’m doing something strange, but that while it’s happening I feel trapped inside it.

At the station, when I kept moving the airway bags around, it wasn’t like I was enjoying it or trying to make some kind of point. I knew everybody could see me, and I knew my lieutenant was getting upset. I heard him tell me to stop and step away. I understood that I was supposed to stop. But the thought of leaving the bags where they were, or breaking the sequence before it was finished, felt… awful. Like the whole bay would go bad somehow if I didn’t get it right.

And I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. That’s what makes it distressing. Because part of me is standing there thinking, “What are you doing? Why are you repeating dispatch numbers? Why can’t you just walk away?” But another part is completely locked onto the pattern and the numbers and this feeling that it has to be right.

So yes, it’s causing me a lot of distress. I don’t want to be that person at work. I don’t want to ignore orders or have people looking at me like I’m unsafe or unreliable. It scares me that something can feel that important in the moment, even when I can’t explain it afterward in a way that makes sense.

It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like they're being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, not because I thought that was a normal thing to do, exactly, but because it felt connected. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.\n\nWhen my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. But it felt imposed, yeah — not by him, but by the whole situation, like if I broke the sequence or left before it was finished, the bay would go bad somehow. Unsafe, wrong, out of order. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are a part of who you are, or do they feel foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. I know I’m the one doing it. I can see my own hands moving the bags, I can hear myself saying the dispatch numbers out loud, so it’s not like I think someone else is physically making me do it. But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you cannot control?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I get thoughts stuck in my head, yes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by recurring thoughts I can’t control.\n\nLike sometimes it’s less of a thought with words and more like a pressure or a certainty that something isn’t right, and then I keep coming back to it even when I’m trying not to. I’ll tell myself, “Just leave it alone,” and then my mind keeps circling back anyway. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding to think about it. It sort of grabs my attention and won’t let me move on.\n\nBut if you mean, like, one specific sentence or image that repeats over and over every day, I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say it that neatly. It’s more like my brain keeps returning to the same problem until I do something about it, and even then sometimes it doesn’t really feel finished. So… yes, maybe, but I’m not totally sure I’m using the right words.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of you or something that is happening to you against your will?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something happening to me, honestly. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, so I don’t want to make it sound like I blacked out or like someone else picked up my arms. But it doesn’t feel like a normal decision either.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, really. At the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern, and at the time it felt like I knew it was the right pattern. Not just that I preferred it or wanted things neat — it was like there was a correct arrangement and I had to get it there. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, over and over, because somehow the numbers felt tied to where the bags were supposed to go.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop and step away, and I heard him. I understood he was giving me an order. But I couldn’t make myself leave it alone, because it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad. Not “bad” in a normal way I can explain, but wrong, unsafe, like the place wouldn’t be right anymore.\n\nSo… no, it doesn’t feel like “me” in the way I think of myself. I don’t want to be standing there ignoring my lieutenant and repeating numbers. But in the moment it feels so strong that it’s almost like it’s happening through me, and I’m trying to keep up with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it is. I mean, if I’m being honest, that’s probably the worst part — not just that I’m doing something strange, but that while it’s happening I feel trapped inside it.\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags around, it wasn’t like I was enjoying it or trying to make some kind of point. I knew everybody could see me, and I knew my lieutenant was getting upset. I heard him tell me to stop and step away. I understood that I was supposed to stop. But the thought of leaving the bags where they were, or breaking the sequence before it was finished, felt… awful. Like the whole bay would go bad somehow if I didn’t get it right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. That’s what makes it distressing. Because part of me is standing there thinking, “What are you doing? Why are you repeating dispatch numbers? Why can’t you just walk away?” But another part is completely locked onto the pattern and the numbers and this feeling that it has to be right.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing me a lot of distress. I don’t want to be that person at work. I don’t want to ignore orders or have people looking at me like I’m unsafe or unreliable. It scares me that something can feel that important in the moment, even when I can’t explain it afterward in a way that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I wouldn’t want to say it that neatly. It’s more like my brain keeps returning to the same problem until I do something about it, and even then sometimes it doesn’t really feel finished. Yeah, it is. I knew everybody could see me, and I knew my lieutenant was getting upset. I heard him tell me to stop and step away. I understood that I was supposed to stop. But the thought of leaving the bags where they were, or breaking the sequence before it was finished, felt… awful. Like the whole bay would go bad somehow if I didn’t get it right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. That’s what makes it distressing. Because part of me is standing there thinking, “What are you doing? Why are you repeating dispatch numbers? Why can’t you just walk away?” But another part is completely locked onto the pattern and the numbers and this feeling that it has to be right.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing me a lot of distress. I don’t want to be that person at work. I don’t want to ignore orders or have people looking at me like I’m unsafe or unreliable. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… no, not really. That’s what bothers me so much about it. It doesn’t feel consistent with who I think I am or what I believe in.

I mean, I work in emergency medical services. The whole point is you follow procedure, you listen to command, you keep the bay and the equipment ready in a practical way. I believe in that. I believe in being reliable and not making things harder for everybody else. So when I’m standing there at the station moving the airway bags around into some pattern because it feels “right,” and repeating the dispatch numbers out loud like they have to match somehow, that’s not… that’s not how I want to behave.

And when my lieutenant told me to stop and step away, normally I would. I respect the chain of command. I understand why that matters. But in that moment, stepping away felt impossible because it felt like if I broke the sequence, the whole bay would go bad — not in a way I can defend logically, but it felt true right then.

So no, I wouldn’t say it matches my values. My values are to be useful, steady, safe, disciplined. What happened felt like I was being pulled away from all of that by something that didn’t make sense but still felt urgent. That’s why it scares me.

I mean… yes, it felt like I had to. It wasn’t just me wanting things neat or trying to be stubborn with my lieutenant. The bags were in the wrong order, and once I noticed it, I couldn’t leave them like that.\n\nI kept thinking there was a right pattern, like the numbers from dispatch fit into it somehow, so I was saying them out loud while I moved the bags. And when he told me to stop, I heard him, I did, but stepping away before the sequence was finished felt… dangerous, I guess. Like the whole bay would go bad or be wrong if I broke it right then. So I just kept doing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when your lieutenant told you to stop arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. It wasn’t like normal anxiety exactly, like being nervous because my boss was mad at me. I knew he was telling me to stop, and I could hear his voice, and part of me knew I was making it worse by not listening. But when he told me to step away from the airway bags, it felt like he was asking me to leave something unfinished that absolutely could not be left that way.\n\nThe pattern was almost there, and I had the dispatch numbers going in my head and out loud, and they matched the way the bags needed to be lined up. So when he interrupted it, I got this really bad feeling in my chest and stomach, like if I stopped right then the bay would… I don’t know, go bad. Like the whole place would be wrong or unsafe somehow. I know that sounds strange, but at the time it felt very real.\n\nSo yes, I was uncomfortable. More than uncomfortable, really. I felt trapped between him telling me to stop and this need to finish the sequence before I could walk away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind before you started arranging the airway bags?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before I started moving them, it was like the numbers were already looping. The dispatch numbers, the ones we’d been hearing and using, they were kind of repeating in my head, not like a song exactly, but like they had an order to them. And then when I looked at the airway bags, the way they were sitting there just looked wrong.\n\nI don’t know if “thoughts” is the right word. It was more like this pressure that the numbers and the bags were connected, and I had to make them match. So I started saying the numbers out loud while I moved the bags, because keeping them only in my head wasn’t enough. It felt like if I didn’t get the sequence right, the whole bay would go bad somehow.\n\nAnd then once I started, I couldn’t just stop halfway. That’s why when my lieutenant told me to step away, I didn’t. I heard him, I knew he was telling me to stop, but the repeating numbers and the pattern weren’t finished yet, and it felt dangerous to break it right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like arranging objects or repeating actions outside of your job as an EMT?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing they sent me here for was at work, with the airway bags. That’s the one everybody keeps talking about, because I kept moving them into this pattern that felt right, like it had to be that way.\n\nI was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, and I know that probably looked strange from the outside, but at the time it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the numbers and the bags were supposed to line up somehow, and if I stopped before it was finished… I don’t know, it felt like the whole bay would go bad. Like something would be off or unsafe if I broke the sequence.\n\nSo when my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him, but I couldn’t step away right then. I wasn’t trying to ignore him just to be difficult. I just felt like if I walked away before it was done, I’d be leaving it wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, not in a way I’ve kept track of. The thing at work is the one that stands out because everybody saw it, and because it became… a whole issue.\n\nAt home, I don’t know. I guess I have preferences about how things are set down or put away, but I don’t know if I’d call them rituals. I’m not sitting there making a list of it, you know? Sometimes something looks wrong and I’ll adjust it, but I can’t tell you if that’s the same kind of thing or just me being particular.\n\nIf you mean, like, do I have some exact routine I have to do every night or a repeated thing I can clearly name, I don’t know that I can give you one. Nothing jumps out at me the way the airway bags did. That was different. That felt like it had to be finished right then. At home, I’m not sure. Maybe I don’t notice it as much because nobody’s standing there telling me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way or repeat certain actions is interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it is interfering. I don’t really like saying that, because it makes it sound like I’m just not trying hard enough or like I’m choosing to be difficult, but… I can’t work the ambulance anymore. That’s the main thing. I’m an EMT, that’s what I do, and now I’m not trusted to be on the truck because I couldn’t leave things alone, or I’d get stuck on checking or arranging or trying to make the equipment feel right.\n\nThey tried putting me on lighter stuff, desk-duty type things, notes, equipment checks, that kind of thing. But even that got messed up. My notes weren’t dependable, and the checks would take too long or I’d second-guess them, or I’d get caught up in whether something was in the right place instead of just finishing the task. Eventually they just took me off the schedule.\n\nAnd outside of work… yeah, it’s affected things. I’ve mostly stopped seeing friends. It’s not like I made some big announcement or anything, I just stopped answering as much, stopped making plans, because I don’t want to explain what’s going on or have people notice how off I am. At home I’m not keeping up with things the way I used to either. Stuff piles up, or I start trying to fix one thing and then I don’t actually get the rest done.\n\nSo yes, I guess the honest answer is it’s not just annoying. It’s kind of taken over more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are something you want to be doing, or do they feel like they're being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel like I want to be doing it. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because when I’m in it, it doesn’t feel like some hobby or preference, like, “Oh, I like the bags lined up this way.” It feels more like… there’s a right version of how things are supposed to be, and once I notice it, I can’t just ignore it.\n\nAt the station, with the airway bags, I kept moving them around because the pattern felt right — like I knew where each one was supposed to go. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, not because I thought that was a normal thing to do, exactly, but because it felt connected. Like the numbers and the bags had to match up somehow.\n\nWhen my lieutenant told me to stop, I heard him. I knew he was telling me to step away. But it felt imposed, yeah — not by him, but by the whole situation, like if I broke the sequence or left before it was finished, the bay would go bad somehow. Unsafe, wrong, out of order. So it wasn’t like I was choosing it because I enjoyed it. It felt like I had to finish it, even though part of me knew I was making everything worse by not stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these patterns and arrangements are a part of who you are, or do they feel foreign to you?\n\nPatient: It feels… not exactly like me, but not completely foreign either, if that makes sense. I know I’m the one doing it. I can see my own hands moving the bags, I can hear myself saying the dispatch numbers out loud, so it’s not like I think someone else is physically making me do it. But it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my personality, like “this is just how I’ve always been.”\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags into that pattern, it felt like I knew the right way they were supposed to be. Not just preferred it — knew it. And once that was in my head, stepping away felt almost impossible. My lieutenant told me to stop, and I understood the words, but I couldn’t just leave it unfinished, because it felt like if I broke the sequence the whole bay would go bad somehow. Like unsafe, or wrong, or… I don’t know, contaminated in a way I couldn’t point to.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it feels foreign in the sense that I don’t recognize myself when I’m doing it. I don’t want to be the guy standing there repeating numbers and ignoring orders. But in the moment it also feels like it belongs to me because I’m the only one who can see how it’s supposed to line up. That’s the part that scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring thoughts that you cannot control?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… I get thoughts stuck in my head, yes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by recurring thoughts I can’t control.\n\nLike sometimes it’s less of a thought with words and more like a pressure or a certainty that something isn’t right, and then I keep coming back to it even when I’m trying not to. I’ll tell myself, “Just leave it alone,” and then my mind keeps circling back anyway. It’s not like I sit there calmly deciding to think about it. It sort of grabs my attention and won’t let me move on.\n\nBut if you mean, like, one specific sentence or image that repeats over and over every day, I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say it that neatly. It’s more like my brain keeps returning to the same problem until I do something about it, and even then sometimes it doesn’t really feel finished. So… yes, maybe, but I’m not totally sure I’m using the right words.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are a part of you or something that is happening to you against your will?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something happening to me, honestly. I mean, I know I’m the one doing it, so I don’t want to make it sound like I blacked out or like someone else picked up my arms. But it doesn’t feel like a normal decision either.\n\nThat’s why they sent me here, really. At the station I kept moving the airway bags around into this pattern, and at the time it felt like I knew it was the right pattern. Not just that I preferred it or wanted things neat — it was like there was a correct arrangement and I had to get it there. And I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it, over and over, because somehow the numbers felt tied to where the bags were supposed to go.\n\nMy lieutenant told me to stop and step away, and I heard him. I understood he was giving me an order. But I couldn’t make myself leave it alone, because it felt like if I broke the sequence or walked away before it was finished, the whole bay would go bad. Not “bad” in a normal way I can explain, but wrong, unsafe, like the place wouldn’t be right anymore.\n\nSo… no, it doesn’t feel like “me” in the way I think of myself. I don’t want to be standing there ignoring my lieutenant and repeating numbers. But in the moment it feels so strong that it’s almost like it’s happening through me, and I’m trying to keep up with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, if I’m being honest, that’s probably the worst part — not just that I’m doing something strange, but that while it’s happening I feel trapped inside it.\n\nAt the station, when I kept moving the airway bags around, it wasn’t like I was enjoying it or trying to make some kind of point.
